# Bug lessons: why the tests missed it, and what catches the whole class

The owner, 2026-10-14: «после каждого найденного бага делать вывод почему существующими тестами мы его не
отловили и как в целом нужно улучшать свой подход к тестам чтобы отлавливать такие классы ошибок. однотипные
баги не хочется каждый раз скидывать».

So for every bug, whoever finds it (the owner, QA, a reviewer, a failing release) adds an entry here and does
three things:

1. **Why the tests missed it.** Name the gap: the path no test took, the state no fixture had, the level
   that cannot see it.
2. **The class.** Give the general kind of mistake, not the instance.
3. **Sweep and guard.** Find every other instance of the class in the code and fix them in the same change.
   Then add the check that catches the class from now on: a property test, a shared helper, an e2e sweep, a
   QA checklist line or a lint rule. A fix that only covers the reported case is not done.

The classes so far are below. Each has its standing rule, and new code is written against all of them.

## Classes

### 1. One decision computed in two places
- **Bug.** The composer's Create button was enabled from the form's title, while the action refused on the
  line's text. A task typed into a form opened by hand was refused with "Type something first." (stage 8).
- **Missed because.** Every test entered tasks the same way, by typing the line and pressing Enter. The two
  predicates agree on that path.
- **Rule.** A yes/no that the UI shows (enabled, visible, allowed) and the action enforces is one function
  in the view-model, read by both. Test it as a property: *whenever the control is enabled, the action
  accepts*, over every way into the state.
- **Rule (across the wire).** The same holds when the two sides are the client and the server: a
  rule the server enforces (e.g. "a session cannot read its own sign-in notice") is one function in
  `core` that the client also reads before it acts. A fix that changes such a rule on one side gets
  the other side traced in its review brief, and a steady-state test (count the requests: nothing
  repeats for the same id, the badge settles). Instance: QA round 15 H1 — the server skipped the
  own-session notice on read, the feed kept re-marking it (~70 requests a second, the bell never
  cleared); the server-side fix and its re-review looked at the server diff only.

### 2. One entry path tested, the others assumed
- **Bug.** Same as 1. Also: the Inbox button counted something Now's counter did not.
- **Missed because.** The tests followed the happy path the author had in mind.
- **Rule.** For every form or flow, list its entry paths: typed, pasted, opened empty, from the assistant,
  from a shortcut, from a notification, editing. Test submit from each. QA opens every form every way
  (docs/qa.md).

### 3. Transient UI state cleared before the store catches up (async gap)
- **Bug.** A checked task flashed back after the dissolve, because "dissolving" was cleared before the close
  was written (stage 8).
- **Missed because.** Tests resolved the action at once; nothing held it pending.
- **Rule.** UI tests of an async action keep it pending with a deferred promise, and assert the screen at
  three moments: pending, then resolved, then refused. Local transient state is released when the store
  reflects the change, not when the call starts.

### 4. A number borrowed from a view-model built for something else
- **Bug.** Projects showed "0" because it read Now's filter chips, which drop projects (stage 8).
- **Missed because.** The fixtures had several projects with open tasks, where the borrowed value happened
  to be right.
- **Rule.** Each figure on a screen comes from a named view-model function for that purpose. Test it with
  edge fixtures: zero, one, all in one group, nothing open.
- **Rule (the reader side).** When a value's source changes (the day's clipped part of a block becomes
  the block itself), every reader of it is traced against the new range, not only the screen that
  showed the old one. A form gives back exactly what it was handed for every field left untouched
  (`keptAt` in the Day sheet's `readDraft`), and a round-trip sweep over lengths, zones and summer-time
  changes pins it. Instance: QA round 17 review I1, below.

### 5. Layout, focus and animation that unit tests cannot see
- **Bug.** The composer's header slid out of its card while it unfolded. A focused input made the browser
  scroll an `overflow: hidden` box (stage 8).
- **Missed because.** jest-expo renders no layout. No e2e test looked at geometry during an animation.
- **Rule.** Anything that animates size or position gets an e2e check that samples the bounding boxes
  during the animation: nothing leaves its container. Every changed screen is looked at in screenshots,
  both themes, at phone and desktop widths.
  A control that replaces the one holding focus (an inline confirm, Cancel giving the button back)
  moves focus onto its successor with `useConfirmFocus()` and says its question; an e2e test walks it
  by keyboard and asserts `toBeFocused()`.
  A control that swaps for its status (an answered notice's "Not me" becoming "Session ended") hands
  focus to that status (`toStatus()` / `statusRef()`), which takes it only if focus is still nowhere.
  A busy button takes focus back only under the same condition (`isFocusLost()`): focus the person
  moved meanwhile stays where it is.

### 6. Overlays placed without knowing what they cover
- **Bug.** Toasts lay over the time bar's buttons. The Calendar explanation appeared as a toast far from
  its button, after a delay (stage 8).
- **Missed because.** No test checks overlap. Toasts were placed at a fixed offset.
- **Rule.** Overlays (toast, tooltip, popover, banner) are positioned against their screen's real chrome or
  anchored to their cause. An e2e sweep shows every overlay on every screen and asserts it covers no
  enabled control. An explanation of a control is a tooltip on that control.

### 7. Invariants that live in folder structure
- **Bug.** Moving Projects, Insights and Settings out of `(tabs)` silently dropped the sign-in guard
  (stage 8, found in review).
- **Missed because.** Route tests ran signed in only.
- **Rule.** Guards and wrappers sit in one central place, not in a parent folder that files can leave. The
  route sweep runs signed out too: every non-public route must land on /login.

### 8. Only initial states tested, never transitions
- **Bug.** The central guard read "onboarded?" once, in a layout that never remounts. After the
  walk-through it would have sent the phone back into it forever (stage 8, found in review).
- **Missed because.** Tests mounted the component in state A or in state B. None changed A to B while it
  was mounted.
- **Rule.** For every hook or component holding state read from storage or the server, test the change
  while mounted: false → true, signed in → signed out, empty → filled.

### 9. Time boundaries
- **Bug.** "Attend" on an event that starts in a few minutes failed with "That time is in the future." The
  reminder and the Calendar button offer events slightly ahead (stage 8).
- **Missed because.** Fixtures used events that had already started.
- **Rule.** Anything taking an instant is tested around its boundaries: before, at and after the start;
  just before and after now; across midnight and a zone change. Prefer fast-check properties over offsets
  relative to `now` instead of fixed timestamps. Run them in a zone other than UTC (the owner's is
  Europe/Moscow, UTC+3) and feed them the input in the shape the platform really hands out (expo-calendar's
  Android module: UTC strings `yyyy-MM-ddTHH:mm:ss.SSSZ`), not only hand-made ISO fixtures. When a report
  names a screen, reproduce on that screen's own path before concluding where the refusal came from: a
  toast is app-wide and outlives a tab switch.

### 10. A control that does nothing on one platform
- **Bug.** "Add a computer" did nothing on phones (stage 8; the root cause is recorded with its fix).
- **Missed because.** e2e runs only the web build. App tests rendered the button but never pressed it on
  the phone layout.
- **Rule.** Every control on a phone-layout screen has an app test that presses it and asserts a visible
  result. The QA heuristic "every control must change something" is checked at phone width too.
  `deadControls()` does this for a whole screen at once; a new main screen joins
  `phone-controls.test.tsx`. A control whose action writes nothing (an empty list of events) is
  dead on every platform: an answer that changes nothing still has to be written down.

### 11. The web build standing in for Android's layout
- **Bug.** On Android, Now's calendar card ("Attend or skip?") was drawn over the first two task rows: the
  rows stayed where they had been before the card arrived, while the list below them had moved down
  (stage 8, the owner's screenshot on v0.9.0).
- **Missed because.** React Native's renderer on Android flattens a View that only lays out (padding, an
  `aria-label`, a `role`): the task list's box vanished, its rows became children of the scroll view, and
  the card's arrival changed every row's own frame, which set off each row's Reanimated layout transition
  (`ROW_LAYOUT`); on the phone they stuck at their start. The web never flattens, so the e2e suite (web
  only) and jest (no layout) could not see it; nobody opened Now on Android with an event on.
- **Rule.** Rows that slide with a layout transition sit in `RowList` (`apps/app/src/ui/row-list.tsx`), a
  box kept a native view (`collapsable={false}`), so nothing outside the list can move a row's own frame.
  The structural app test `rowsInFlattenableBoxes()` (apps/app/src/test/sliding-rows.ts) runs on every
  screen with such rows; a card that arrives above a list gets an e2e geometry check at phone width (card
  and first row do not intersect), and the QA round on Android opens screens whose cards load late.
- **Rule (units).** One unit system: every size is a px/dp token. The Tailwind theme (spacing, sizes,
  type, radius) is generated from `tokens.json` in px (`scripts/design/generate-design.ts`), Tailwind's
  rem scales are replaced, and NativeWind's `inlineRem` is pinned at 16 so a rem that slips through is
  the same size on both platforms. The generator test reads `metro.config.js` and fails without it
  (F-AND-01: rem compiled to 14 px on Android, so every box was 12.5 % smaller than on the web while
  font sizes in px were not).
- **Rule (offsets).** A negative margin that lets a hit target overhang its visual is half the
  difference of two sizes (`(target − visual) / 2`); it is chosen together with both sizes, never
  rounded on its own. Pick the target from the size tokens so the difference lands on the scale
  (a 32 pill in a 48 target: `-my-2`). Centring the target in a box of the visual's size works on the web only: Android does not deliver touches outside a parent's bounds, so the overhang there is dead (the close crosses on ≥ 1024 dp windows, open for Task 3.9). Instance:
  Task 2.2's rounding gave chips `-my-2` around a 32 pill in a 44 target, so each pill stuck out of
  its layout box by 2 px, and the progress marker sat 2 px off the bar's centre.
- **Rule (mirrors).** A JS number that restates a class (a row height a layout sum subtracts, a padding
  a height cap adds, a line height a "N lines" cap multiplies) moves with that class: derive it from the
  tokens where one exists (`typeRole(...)`), and pin it with an app test that reads the class through the
  generated scale (`classPx` / `classLinePx` in `apps/app/src/test/class-px.ts`; jest renders class
  names, not styles), never one that repeats the same arithmetic with the same constant. Instance (batch
  1+2 close-out): Task 2.2 rounded Week's day-names row and gutter from `h-7`/`pt-7` (28, off the
  generated scale) to `h-8`/`pt-8`, while `LABELS_PX = 28` stayed; with night hours on at 390 × 844 the
  24 hours took the 4 px the fit had counted as free and the grid scrolled. The jest test said 26 px an
  hour because it computed 624 / 24 with the same 28. The same task turned the composer's 24 px lines
  into the 20 px `callout` line, and its "eight lines" cap (212) became nine and a half.

### 12. A producer's output never checked against its own contract
- **Bug.** `/api/parse` could answer with an activity label longer than 80 characters, a summary
  longer than 500, an expected length past a day, or a new task with over 100 subtasks. The schema
  the client parses the answer with refuses all of these, so the whole answer was lost. The client
  also refused answers with extra keys, so any key a newer server added would break an older app
  (stage 8, Task 11 review).
- **Missed because.** The API tests read answers with `json<T>()`, a cast that checks nothing.
  Fixtures used short, tidy texts. Nothing fed the producers long or odd input.
- **Rule.** Whatever a server sends goes through the endpoint's own `output` schema in its tests.
  A fast-check property feeds the producers (rules, model conversion, summaries, planners) random
  long texts and answers, and asserts the output still fits the schema. Limits live in one place
  (`proposals/limits.ts`), read by both the schema and the producers. Response schemas are loose
  (`z.object`); only request input is strict.

### 13. A contract strict in the direction that must stay open
- **Bug.** The calendar answer was a strict schema shared with what the phone sends: adding the
  event's details to the answer would have made every older client refuse it (stage 8, found in
  review).
- **Missed because.** Client and server always shipped together in the tests; no test parsed an answer
  from a newer server.
- **Rule.** What a client sends is strict; what a server answers is not (unknown keys are dropped;
  class 12 made the parse answer loose by hand, and the LLM status answer was still missed). One
  place decides it now: `endpoint()` loosens every output (`loosen` in
  `packages/core/src/api/loose.ts`: every object at any depth, all other checks kept), so a schema
  may stay strict where it also checks an input. `endpoints.test.ts` walks every endpoint's output
  and fails on any strict object at any depth.
- **Also open: what a newer server can add a value to.** Unknown keys are not the only way in: a new
  kind of notification, a new resolution, push method or delivery result fails a closed enum or a
  discriminated union just as hard, and one such entry lost the whole list. A list of things a
  server can grow is read with `knownValues` (unknown entries dropped, the rest kept), a single
  value with `knownValue` (a stand-in for an unknown one) or `knownOrNull`
  (`packages/core/src/api/tolerant.ts`). A socket message is an answer too: it is read through
  `loosen`. `loosen` and the sweep walk records as well.
- **And what an older server leaves out.** A field added to an answer is absent from an older
  server's (a self-hosted one, a stale deploy): it is read with `knownOrNull` (absent reads as
  `null`) or as optional, never as required (stage 8 sweep: `fcmProject`). An added capability
  flag defaults to what older servers actually did; name the flag and the reason in this class:
  `features.mcp` (`packages/core/src/api/schemas/instance.ts`) defaults to true, because every
  server served MCP before one could be without it (an older http server failed then as it does
  now), and false would hide a working Connect URL on every older https server (PR A fix round 2
  N4; review M3 accepted the default).

### 14. A control inside a control
- **Bug.** Day's block row is one button (it opens the edit sheet); a block on a task carried the
  task's title as a link inside that button. A screen reader never reached the link, the web build
  nested a focusable inside a focusable (axe `nested-interactive`), and the link was 15 px tall
  (`target-size`) (stage 8, found by the Task picker's e2e, which is the first to put a block on a
  task before running axe on Day).
- **Missed because.** Day's tests and its axe pass ran on blocks without a task; the link only shows
  for a block on a task. jest cannot see the nesting either: it finds no link by role inside an
  accessible button, and no test looked for one.
- **Rule.** A row that is pressable as a whole holds no other control: anything else it offers
  (a link, Stop, a check) sits beside it or on its own line. `controlsInsideControls()`
  (apps/app/src/test/nested-controls.ts) runs on screens with such rows, with the fixture that
  shows every optional part (a block on a task, something running), and the e2e axe pass runs with
  that fixture too.

### 15. An outside service's refusal read by its status alone, with a lasting consequence
- **Bug.** Any 401 or 403 from FCM marked push broken for every user until the key changed. FCM
  answers 403 `SENDER_ID_MISMATCH` for one phone's token issued by another Firebase project, and
  401 for an access token it stopped taking: one odd device, or one stale token, would have
  switched push off for everyone, for good (stage 8, Task 8 review).
- **Missed because.** The test double answered bare statuses with empty bodies, so the code
  could only ever look at the status; nobody read the provider's error codes.
- **Rule.** Classify a provider's refusal by its documented error body first (FCM's `FcmError`
  `errorCode` and the field it names), the status only after that. A refusal about one item (a
  device's token) acts on that item; a stale credential is refreshed and retried once before
  anything is concluded. A state that turns something off for everyone expires and is checked
  again (`BROKEN_FOR_MS`). Test doubles answer with the provider's real error bodies.

### 16. Each side checks it has the thing, nobody checks that both have the same one
- **Bug.** The app chose Google's push whenever the server offered FCM and the phone had a Google
  token. An APK built with one Firebase project's `google-services.json` and a server holding
  another project's service account (a self-hosted server) get `SENDER_ID_MISMATCH` on every
  send: the server dropped the phone as gone, the app registered FCM again on its next start, and
  no notification ever arrived, silently (stage 8, the owner).
- **Missed because.** Each side was tested alone: the app against a server that "offers fcm",
  the server against tokens that were all its own project's or plainly dead. No test paired two
  identities, and the refusal that means "never with this server" was read as "gone".
- **Rule.** When two parts must be configured from the same source (a Firebase project, a bot, a
  key pair, an origin), the handshake names the identity, and each side compares it before it
  relies on the other (`fcmProject` in `GET /api/push/methods`, `projectId` in the FCM
  registration). A provider's refusal of the pairing is its own outcome (`incompatible`), never
  "gone": the device moves to what works (polling) at once, and the server remembers the pairing
  (`fcm_refused_tokens`) so a retry cannot start a silent loop. Test the whole path: refused →
  fallback → the next start stays on the fallback.

### 17. A failed external action has no persisted path back to a retry
- **Bug.** Telegram and FCM send refusals were logged as failures, but the pool pass never revisited their existing notifications. FCM dismissals were marked complete before FCM accepted them, so a refused dismissal had no retry path.
- **Missed because.** Notification integration tests covered successful sends, permanent refusals and repeated passes after success; none ran a pool alarm after a transient refusal or inspected an unsuccessful dismissal.
- **Rule.** A transient refusal leaves a durable pending state and arms the next alarm. Commit delivery or dismissal only after transport acceptance. Test refusal, alarm retry, and a later pass that must not duplicate success for every external channel.

### 18. A permanent provider refusal treated as a transient one
- **Bug.** Telegram's permanent HTTP 400 deletion refusal kept an already resolved notification on a one-minute retry alarm forever. FCM dismissal also collapsed a project mismatch or refused credentials into a transient failure.
- **Missed because.** The retry tests used only HTTP 500 followed by success. They did not run another alarm after a provider response that cannot become successful for that copy.
- **Rule.** Preserve provider refusal classes through the transport, persist terminal dismissal outcomes without claiming the copy was removed, and exclude them from the retry queue. Test one later pool pass for each terminal channel outcome.

- **Rule (the opposite case, review I2 of the PR #48 follow-ups).** A fix that stops retrying the permanent case must not catch the transient one: classify by what the answer says (a 4xx about this request), never by what it lacks (no retry time). The test runs both: N refusals end the retries, and N outages of any length do not.

The same rule applies when a previously successful channel is no longer configured: a delivered Telegram copy cannot be deleted without a bot transport. Record `dismissal-unavailable` with `dismissedAt` still null and stop the minute retry alarm, as for a missing FCM transport.

- **Accepted residual (re-review N8 of PR A).** A 400 every provider answers because of our own
  configuration (a bad parameter after an SDK change, a model renamed) reads as a refusal for good:
  each text then queued goes to the Inbox after three drains (about 15 minutes), and a first read
  answers `unreadable` (N7). Accepted: the Inbox keeps the person's words, nothing is lost, and the
  warning log names the provider and why for every refusal (`noteFailure`), so a config
  mistake shows as a wall of 400s there. Telling "this request" from "every request" would need a
  second, known-good probe per refusal, which costs a model call for every refused text.

### 19. Progress saved for one session reused in another
- **Bug.** The phone stored one push poll cursor for all accounts. After account A reached sequence 100, account B's first poll sent `after=100`; the server discarded B's earlier pending entries. The phone usage upload cursor likewise skipped history when the account changed.
- **Missed because.** Tests only repeated polling or uploading within one signed-in session. They never switched sessions while keeping the phone's secure storage, and the fake server did not make one account's cursor larger than another's queue. Later tests delayed only the response, missing a switch between the last local read and the API call; they also made only one poll at a time. The platform tests filled SecureStore directly, while the app-state test used an injected memory session store, exposing an accidental dependence on the storage adapter.
- **Rule.** Every persisted server progress marker carries the identity of the session whose data it advances. A different session, or an old marker without identity, starts from the beginning. Capture session identity from the API client's in-memory credential, which is the one `call` sends, independently of where auth persists it. Before an API call, synchronously compare that credential after all awaited preparation; check it again before applying the answer. If an asynchronous display finishes after a switch, take it back. Advance an upload cursor only after every batch was sent. Serialise concurrent progress reads and writes for one session so a later dismissal and cursor cannot be overtaken. Test A → B during preparation, response and display, and two overlapping polls whose responses change the same notification.

### 20. A test acts before server settings reach the screen
- **Bug.** Week's Day navigation pressed Previous day before the first sync applied the seeded UTC zone. The displayed date changed from 15 to 13 across the zone switch, so a counting loop missed 7 October.
- **Missed because.** The e2e test assumed the seed's time zone was already visible when the page loaded. It did not wait for the app to receive and apply the server's settings before navigating dates.
- **Rule.** A scenario that depends on a server setting waits for that setting to appear in the UI before acting on derived dates or state. Check the authoritative value first; Week's test waits for Settings to show `Account: UTC` before Day navigation.

### 21. A one-tap destructive action has no recovery path
- **Bug.** History's Undo button revoked an event on the first press, though that correction cannot itself be undone. The Done check on Now and Project rows and the task detail also closed a task with no immediate way back.
- **Missed because.** The History test checked that Undo existed but never pressed it. The Done tests checked that the task closed, not whether an accidental press could be reversed.
- **Rule.** A destructive one-tap action must offer immediate recovery; if recovery is not available, it needs explicit confirmation before the write. Test the first tap, Cancel or recovery, and the final write. History uses the shared Sheet confirmation (phone sheet, desktop dialog, Escape/backdrop dismissal, no primary hotkey). Done uses the shared `useCloseWithUndo` action and an Undo toast. Sweep direct `actions.revoke` controls and other destructive actions whenever adding one.

### 22. Account preferences used before there is an account
- **Bug.** A signed-out Russian browser saw an English login because `useT` read the empty local account's English default; the HTML document also kept `lang=en` after Russian account settings arrived.
- **Missed because.** Login tests used the English test locale and screen tests did not inspect the document language.
- **Rule.** Before sign-in, visible language follows the device; after sign-in it follows account settings. Both transitions update the web document's language. Sweep all signed-out and crash screens that translate text. Test a Russian guest and an English account in the same mounted runtime; e2e verifies the guest HTML language.
- **Document variant (F-WEB-02, Task 2.3).** The same shape one layer up: the page itself (`<html>`: ground, `color-scheme`, `theme-color`) kept to the OS while the app followed the person's saved theme, so a dark-theme user on a light phone got a white flash (and a light status bar) before the bundle mounted and again behind every sheet. Missed because: every e2e and jest test looked at the React tree after mount, in the theme the test had just chosen, and `#f4f5f7`/`#0b0b0c` were hand-typed in `index.html`, the manifest and the icon script, where no token change could reach them. Rule: whatever the browser paints before React (HTML, CSS, a boot script, the manifest, native splash) takes its colours from the tokens through the generator (`bun design:generate` writes them; `bun lint` fails when they are stale), and the document is written by one owner at a time: `public/theme-boot.js` before the bundle, `DocumentTheme` after. The first render reads the stored preference synchronously, so the boot value is never overwritten with the OS's for a frame. Guards: `e2e/theme.e2e.ts` (with the bundle blocked, every preference x OS combination paints the app's colour; the theme never flips through the OS's on start; Settings toggles and open sheets update the metas) and `scripts/design/theme-boot.test.ts` (the page's literals equal the tokens' ground; no other hex in the page, the boot script or the manifest).

### 23. A secret embedded in a convenience command
- **Bug.** The one-line computer installer put its five-year device bearer token in `--token`, exposing it in shell history and process arguments.
- **Missed because.** Command tests asserted the exact generated line and the agent tests passed the token as an argument; neither checked where the secret would persist while a real person installs it.
- **Rule.** Setup commands must contain only non-secret configuration. Show the one-time credential separately and read it from a hidden interactive prompt; reject an absent prompt without saving settings. Sweep generated commands, CLI options and docs for other embedded credentials. Test that every platform command omits the token and that the installer saves a prompted token without echoing it.

### 24. Device state survives an account switch
- **Bug.** Android could show a delayed notification from account A after signing in to B, and calendar upload, dismissed prompts and repeating-event rules carried across the account switch. A late upload could restore a calendar copy after its switch was turned off; a delayed FCM show could restore a resolved notice after its dismissal.
- **Missed because.** Push tests used one session and assumed JavaScript handled the FCM payload before native presentation. The calendar test checked off/on within one account.
- **Rule.** A system notification and private phone memory must name the session that owns it. Firebase data messages carry no native `title`/`message`; JavaScript validates a session hash before showing or withdrawing them, and keeps a durable dismissal marker against reordered delivery. The server withdraws a sent copy through its original transport even after the session changes push method. Clear only the old session's presented copies at logout. A new session starts with calendar upload off; serialize upload and clear, and recheck the choice after device reads. Test A → B, push-method switches and Off while a push or read is in flight, and inspect the Android tray.

### 25. Model-suggested account references accepted without checking current state
- **Bug.** An assistant task could name a project absent from the account. Approval created it with the task, even when that was not what the owner intended (PR 2).
- **Missed because.** Tests expected the model's unknown project name to become `project.created` and checked only that the name was present in the user's text, not that it belonged to the account's project list. The fallback rules and approval path had the same assumption.
- **Rule.** An assistant may refer only to existing account entities. The model converter, rule fallback and approval planner each resolve names or IDs against the current active state; unknown references are omitted from previews or refused on approval. Test both named and ID references, including an unknown name in a model answer and in typed fallback text. Manual project creation remains a separate explicit action.

### 26. A parser treats words of a short title as disposable metadata
- **Bug.** A leading «ДЗ» was masked as a category tag, and a model could turn «Написать челику» into the task «челику» with «Написать» as description (PR 2).
- **Missed because.** The quick-input tests explicitly expected the shortened homework title; model conversion tests covered long pasted homework and structured fields, not short one-line titles with a model-shortened answer.
- **Rule.** A short one-line task name with no explicit metadata stays the complete title the owner typed. Category inference must not erase its words. The assistant may put text in the description when there is a separate details line or other clear structure. Test the same title through the rule parser and model conversion, including RU and EN homework words and an imperative verb.

### 27. A picker saves its value but leaves its open state behind
- **Bug.** Picking a task deadline saved the date but kept the calendar open; tapping another field left it open too (PR 2).
- **Missed because.** The date panel's tests covered conversion and form values, but never checked dismissal after a choice or an outside press.
- **Rule.** Every temporary picker must close after a final choice and on an outside press. Check both the value and visibility in a form-level test, and inspect sibling pickers for the same missed transition. Day's calendar and the recurrence end picker already closed on selection; the task's due, start and fixed final date share the repaired panel.

### 28. An optional task reference is mistaken for a command on a task
- **Bug.** Unlinking an activity sent `activity.labelled` with `taskId: null`; the generic retro validator treated every payload with a `taskId` key as a task command and rejected it with `task/unknown`.
- **Missed because.** Core validation tests covered task commands and activity events separately, but not an activity event carrying an optional task link. The sheet tests had no unlink case.
- **Rule.** Classify an event by its command kind before applying task command rules. Sweep all event payloads with `taskId`; only `task.*` and focus commands use this validator. Test both linked and unlinked activity corrections through core and the sheet.

### 29. External handoff waits exist only in a mounted screen
- **Bug.** Reloading `/login` after opening the Telegram bot lost the nonce and returned to the idle login card. The account Wipe/Delete Telegram confirmation had the same reload gap.
- **Missed because.** Login and Settings tests kept each screen mounted through the external action; they did not remount with a pending server request.
- **Rule.** A short-lived external request keeps its opaque ID and expiry in tab storage before leaving the page, restores its poll on remount, and clears the ID on success, cancellation or expiry. Test the storage lifetime and remount of each Telegram wait.

### 30. An assistant failure silently keeps an unclassified activity
- **Bug.** The released activity bar first wrote a typed label under Other, then silently ignored a failed or non-start model answer. The rules also inferred category only from an exact earlier label, so a first «ем» remained Other. The PR 2 preview initially hid both in-flight reading and local fallback source.
- **Missed because.** Tests supplied a successful model answer and checked the resulting activity; they did not assert what the person sees while waiting or after an API failure. The rules tests had no short meal phrase or earlier automatic Other case.
- **Rule.** Every assistant entry shows reading and either a reviewable result or an explicit refusal; fallback cards identify their rule source. Classify clear food phrases without rewriting the label and guard both network failure and unavailable provider responses. Sweep the task composer and follow-up path for silent loss.

### 31. A new nested state field bypasses the RPC boundary
- **Bug.** The new series state carried a template with opaque `overrides` values. `toRpcState` sanitized task overrides but copied series unchanged, so Durable Object RPC inferred `read()` and `apply()` as non-callable and could forward a non-serializable value.
- **Missed because.** Series tests checked sync events, not the whole materialized state returned across RPC. The first full typecheck exposed `never` at unrelated readers.
- **Rule.** When adding a slice to `CoreState`, inspect its nested `unknown` fields and map them to validated, serializable shapes at every RPC boundary. The sweep found task overrides already handled and series template overrides missing. Test both valid and invalid series overrides at `toRpcState`, plus a real Durable Object read after creating a series.

### 32. A multi-event user action writes its first half before validating the rest
- **Bug.** Moving an activity and linking it to a nonexistent task returned an error after the move event was already stored. A batch of assistant actions could also delete a target and then plan an update against the reducer's provisional state, which had not applied the revocation.
- **Missed because.** The tests checked successful edits and single-action failures, but not the event log after a later step failed or the effect of corrections inside one batch.
- **Rule.** Validate every event and reference before dispatching any part of one user action. For provisional assistant batches, track revocations explicitly. The sweep covered client action emission and core batch planning. Guard with tests that inspect the log after a refused activity edit and refuse update-after-delete batches for tasks and activities.

### 33. A derived template reads the creation event after the entity was edited
- **Bug.** Starting a recurring series from an edited task copied its original title, deadline and estimate from `task.created`, losing later changes.
- **Missed because.** Series tests started from a freshly created task. They never edited the source task before turning it into a series.
- **Rule.** A new derived object must read the current materialized entity, not a historical creation payload, unless the product explicitly asks for the original. The sweep checked series templates and other task-derived drafts; the regression edits every copied field before starting a series.

### 34. A preview survives a change to the text it describes
- **Bug.** Editing a task or activity input after an assistant preview left the old Approve card on screen; an in-flight response could also restore it.
- **Missed because.** Tests approved the same text they had submitted and never edited it while the request was pending.
- **Rule.** Invalidate both the visible preview and the in-flight request when its source changes. The sweep covered the Now composer and activity input; tests change text after a preview and during a delayed response.

### 35. A confirmation reports an outcome without owning its state transition
- **Bug.** Two concurrent Wipe requests could both remain pending. Cancel could report success after Confirm had claimed the request for processing, and Settings repeated that false success without checking the response. Expiry could also report `expired` after its conditional update lost to Confirm.
- **Missed because.** Confirmation tests used one sequential request and checked only the happy path. No test overlapped requests or observed a conditional update losing a race; none passed a stale row to the expiry path.
- **Rule.** Replace old requests and insert the new one in one database transaction. Report Cancel or Expired only when the conditional update affected a row; otherwise read the current status. Let the UI follow the returned state. The sweep covered web and bot cancellation, concurrent creation, expiry and the existing confirmation claim; integration and app regressions cover these races.

### 36. A one-shot write is guarded only by React state
- **Bug.** Two Approve presses before rerender each applied the same assistant action and created two tasks.
- **Missed because.** Tests pressed Approve once, then awaited the result. React's `busy` state was never challenged before its next render.
- **Rule.** A write that must run once from one preview needs a synchronous ref latch as well as disabled UI state. In a test, invoke the press twice inside one render batch and assert one recorded result. The sweep found the manual task create path already uses `isAddingRef`; both task and activity assistant approvals share the repaired hook.
- **Rule (across remounts).** A one-shot taken from something that outlives the component (the phone's
  last notification tap, a share kept by a provider above the route gate, a query in the address) is
  marked taken where it lives, not in the component: the source is cleared
  (`clearLastNotificationResponse`, `resetShareIntent`, the query dropped) or the taken key is kept at
  module scope. A per-instance ref or state forgets on every remount, and the route gate, the versions
  screen and a log-out remount whatever sits under them. Test it by remounting: log out, let the
  versions screen give way, start signed out, and assert the action ran once and the path settles.

### 37. A UI change lands without the suites that pin its layout
- **Bug.** PR 2's checkpoint commits moved the time bar from Day back to Now, split Now's list into "With a deadline" and "No deadline", replaced Week's where-it-sat line with the apps used during a block and made a preset chip open the new preset. Five app tests and thirteen e2e tests still asserted the old layout, and one e2e step reloaded the page where it used to navigate, dropping the toast it then checked.
- **Missed because.** The checkpoint commits bypassed the hooks, and the hooks run only lint and unit tests anyway: app and e2e suites run in CI, which a draft pushed with known failures did not gate. Most e2e tests found Now's rows through the one list name "Tasks", which exists only while the tasks are all dated or all undated.
- **Rule.** A change to where a control lives or how a list is grouped runs `bun test:app` and the e2e files that visit that screen before it is committed, not at the end of the PR. E2e steps reach Now's rows through `nowTasks(page)`, never a list name tied to one grouping. A step that checks transient UI (a toast, a sheet) moves within the app, never by `page.goto` or a reload.

### 38. A deterministic id built from a count that History can lower
- **Bug.** Recurring instances and their corrections were named from the series' revision count. Undoing a schedule edit in History lowered the count, so the next edit reused an id already in the log: the derive saw it and wrote nothing, and the upcoming instance stayed on the old schedule for good.
- **Missed because.** Series tests edited forward only. No test undid an edit and edited again, the one path where a count repeats.
- **Rule.** An id that makes a derived write idempotent is built from something that never repeats (the id of the event that caused it), or picks the first unused suffix under a cap, with the decision to write taken from state, not from the id existing. Test edit → undo → edit for every derived write.

### 39. A platform signal read as the person's action
- **Bug.** The sleep guess counted a quiet evening as sleep (one "night" from 15:15 to 02:36 with 218 "wake-ups") and dropped the real night after a few minutes on the phone at 02:27 (owner, 2026-10-26). It read the screen going on as "the person woke up": notifications light the screen every few minutes, so the screen-off stretches of a quiet evening were glued together by blips of five minutes or less, while a real night wake-up of six minutes broke the night in two and the shorter half was thrown away.
- **Missed because.** The fixtures had a screen-on only where a person used the phone. No fixture had notification wakes, a sparse evening or a wake-up longer than the blip limit, and nothing was checked against a real week of the owner's phone use.
- **Rule.** A platform event is what the platform did, not what the person did: a screen lit is not a pickup, a resumed activity of the alarm is not use, a delivered notification is not a read. Decide which event really means the person acted (here the lock screen going away, an app in front, Pace opening) and test with the noise the platform adds (notification wakes, a sparse evening, a long night wake-up). Tune a guess against the owner's real data (production `usage_sessions` plus confirmed sleep blocks), not only against invented nights, and keep a property that a day of frequent use never yields one.

### 40. An integration swapped for a "compatible" one loses a capability without a word
- **Bug.** After PR 2 replaced the Groq provider with a generic OpenAI-compatible one, production showed "Assistant unavailable" on every message (owner, 2026-10-31). The compatible provider sends a JSON schema only when told the endpoint supports structured outputs; without the flag it asked for bare JSON, the model's answer did not fit the schema, and the failure was folded into "every provider is out" with nothing in the logs. The key was fine. Gemini's OpenAI layer then refused the schema itself (400) over its length limits.
- **Missed because.** Every test ran the in-process fake, which answers in shape whatever the request says; nothing checked what goes over the wire, and nothing called the real endpoint before release (the regression runner does not exist). The error path dropped the cause, so even production gave no hint.
- **Rule.** For every external integration, test the request it sends for the capability we rely on (a stubbed `fetch` that inspects the body: schema present, streaming on), not only the parsing of a happy answer. Log each failure with its status and the start of the body. After changing a provider, its config or its schema, run the real endpoints once (all configured slots) before the release, and write the result in the PR.
### 41. A producer's field carried end to end and used nowhere
- **Bug.** The model answers each question with up to six `options`; the schema checked them, the API passed
  them on, and neither the app's cards nor the bot showed them. A question could only be answered by typing a
  correction, Approve gave no hint that it ignores open questions, and the composer's older reading chips sent
  the bare option ("Tomorrow") without the question it answered (QA round 11 M1).
- **Missed because.** Tests asserted the question's text and stopped there: no test asked what each field of
  the answer is for, and the fixtures' options were never looked at. Schema and contract tests prove a field
  arrives, not that anyone reads it.
- **Rule.** Every field a producer (the model, a server answer) sends has a named consumer, or it is dropped
  from the contract. A field meant for the person (options, a hint, a field label) has a UI test on each
  surface that shows it (app and bot) that acts on it, not only renders it. An answer the person gives to a
  question goes as one shared text (`answerText` in `@pace/core`), so every surface sends the same correction.
### 42. A record written for the machine shown to the person as it is
- **Bug.** The assistant log showed every row as "Assistant · parse.task.create · read" with "Parsed by compatible; every field matched the text." under it: the decision's rule key, the provider's internal name and an English sentence the Worker writes for debugging, and neither the message typed nor what became of it (QA round 11 M4).
- **Missed because.** The screen test checked that the row rendered the record's fields, not that a person could read them; the fixture was a notification row with a tidy explanation. Nobody asked "what did the assistant do with my message?" of the screen, in Russian.
- **Rule.** A log, history or audit screen words each record through a view-model in the reader's language (`decisionView`): what was done in words, the person's own text quoted verbatim, the outcome, and machine names (rule keys, provider or model ids, codes) never, or only as a small mapped detail (`providerLabel`). Store the structure the words need (`steps`, `provider`), not only a sentence written at the time; read older rows with a fallback. The screen test asserts the raw identifiers are absent.
### 43. A setting readable only in the form that sets it
- **Bug.** QA round 11 M2: a task's repeat rule and the final deadline of a two-stage deadline showed only inside the Edit sheet. The task screen listed Start, Due, Work left and Tracked, and Now's rows looked the same for a series instance and a one-off, so the person could not tell them apart without opening Edit.
- **Missed because.** The series and deadline tests checked what the actions wrote and what the form read back (open Edit, see the value), never what the task screen or a row shows afterwards. The lived-in seed behind the route sweep had no series at all, so no sweep or QA screenshot ever showed a series instance.
- **Rule.** Every value a person sets in a form is readable without reopening that form: on the screen of the thing it belongs to and, when it changes what the thing is (a series, a second deadline stage), as a mark where the thing is listed. The words come from one pure core function (`describeRepeatRule`, `finalDeadlineOf`) so every surface says the same. Test it on the screen, not in the form. The sweep of the task's Edit sheet: title, description, link, start, due, estimate, subtasks, category and project already showed; deadline type and repeat now do (`task-summary.tsx`, the row's repeat mark and "final deadline …" once the main due date passed). The lived-in seed now has a monthly series with a second stage; `e2e/repeats.e2e.ts` checks the mark and the lines in both themes at 390 and 1440; docs/qa.md's "State you can see" asks for it.

### 44. An ended record read as live
- **Bug.** QA round 13 H1: stopping a task's repeat archives its series, but the task keeps the series id and the series stays in the state. The Edit sheet read the schedule straight from `state.series.byId[task.seriesId]`, so it showed the old repeat after the screen had dropped it. Picking the same kind again changed nothing and the sheet closed as if saved; another kind went to `updateSeries`, which refuses an archived series, after the earlier steps (the final deadline) were already written. `startSeriesForTask` refused too, because the task "had" a series.
- **Missed because.** The repeat tests went one way: start a series, stop it. None started it again after stopping, the one path where the leftover id matters. The sheet's pre-check covered only the last repeat date, not the series actions' other refusals.
- **Rule.** An entity that can end (an archived series, preset or project) is read through one helper that answers "is it live" (`activeSeriesOf`), never through its id or the raw map. Every write path's refusals are a pure predicate that the form checks before its first write and the action refuses through (`seriesStartProblem`, `repeatChangeProblem`), and the form names the field. Test stop → start again and every refusal path for a partial write.

### 45. One word for two actions on one screen
- **Bug.** In Russian the assistant card's Cancel (drop this reading) and the toast's Undo (take back what was just written) both read «Отменить», a thumb apart on a phone after an approval; the rest of the app says «Отмена» for Cancel (QA round 14 M2).
- **Missed because.** The card had its own `assistant.cancel` key, translated on its own; catalog tests checked that every key exists and has the same placeholders, not that one meaning keeps one word, and no test put the card and the toast on screen together in Russian.
- **Rule.** A common action has one key (`common.cancel`, `common.undo`) and screens reuse it rather than adding a copy; Cancel and Undo read differently in every language. `i18n.test.ts` fails when any key that reads "Cancel" or "Undo" in English reads otherwise than the common key in Russian, or when the two common words are the same.

Extended (F-CPY-02/03/05, 2026-11-06): the same class held for «Отменить» (Undo) against «Отменить задачу», "Done" for a task and an approved proposal, and "Revoke" against "End this session?". `glossary.test.ts` now also compares `common.undo` with `review.action.cancel` (RU) and `assistant.approved` with `task.done`, and bans "Revoke" and "Log in" in the English catalog.

### 46. A workflow expression GitHub holds instead of running
- **Bug.** After #45 every Deploy run (after CI and by hand) ended `action_required` with no job started, so the merge never reached production. The new step read all secrets at once (`toJSON(secrets)`) to find the `LLM_*_N` slots; GitHub holds such a run for approval.
- **Missed because.** Workflows have no tests: the jq was simulated locally and the YAML parsed, but nothing runs a workflow before it is on `main`, and nobody checked what GitHub does with an expression over the whole secrets context.
- **Rule.** Name every secret a workflow reads; never `toJSON(secrets)` or `toJSON(vars)`. A workflow change that touches secrets is tried on its branch with a harmless dispatchable workflow using the same pattern (here `llm-regression.yml`) before merging. `bun lint` runs `scripts/check-workflows.ts`, which refuses the whole-context expressions.
### 47. A security notice the one it reports can reach
- **Bug.** Review of the sign-in notices (Task 5): the session a notice reports had full scope, so right after signing in it could resolve or read its own notice, which deleted the Telegram message and dismissed the phone notification; Not me then answered "Seen" and ended nothing. The deep link's reply was also adopted in whatever chat `/start` came from, so in a group the address and place would have been edited into a group message.
- **Missed because.** The tests answered the notice from the person's side only (Telegram, the feed of another session, Settings) and sent `/start` from a private chat only; none acted as the newly signed-in session or from a group.
- **Rule.** A notice that asks the person to judge a session is answered, read and shown only where the person is: never by or to the session it reports (`isOwnSignIn`, one core function the server's refusal `notification/own-sign-in`, the read filter, the push fan-out and the feed share), and only in the person's private chat (the login deep link is not taken in a group; an adopted message must be in the account's chat). Test as the reported session and from a group.

### 48. A session checked once, at the door of a channel that stays open
- **Bug.** Review of phase C Task 7: the live socket checked the bearer only at the upgrade. Revoking the session (Settings, logging out, "Not me") deleted its D1 row and nothing else, so the socket stayed open. On `pace.v1` it went on hearing `changed` and the pool's notices, which include the sign-in notices with address and place. On `pace.v2` it also got every new event, could `pull` the whole log and could `push`. An intruder ended with "Not me" kept writing.
- **Missed because.** Every HTTP route checks the session on each request, and the tests revoked sessions only through HTTP calls (`/api/me` answering 401). No test kept a socket open across a revoke or past an expiry. The socket gaining write power did not prompt anyone to revisit when it was authorized.
- **Rule.** A long-lived channel lives no longer than the session that opened it.
  - The live route hands the store the session id and expiry in headers it sets itself, and the socket carries them in its tags (`live-sessions.ts`).
  - Every revoke goes through `deleteSession`, which closes that session's sockets with `LIVE_SESSION_ENDED` (4401) whether or not the row was still there. Account deletion closes them all.
  - An expired socket is closed instead of served: on its next message, broadcast or announce (`servedSockets` / `isServed`).
  - A request with no session named is refused.
  - On 4401 the client stops reconnecting and checks its session (`me`), so a 401 signs it out.
  - Test: revoke from each path while a socket is open (`live.int.test.ts`: Settings, Not me, logout, expiry, forged headers).
  - Sweep: the live socket is the only channel that outlasts a request. MCP and the OAuth tokens are checked per request, and agent tokens cannot open a socket.
- **Instance (QA round 22 R22-02), the client side: a device that ends its own session keeps
  using it.** Log out told the server first and forgot the token after: the server ends the session
  and closes its socket (4401) before it answers, so a socket close, a sync or a session check set
  off in that gap went out with the revoked token, and a check's 401 called the person's own sign-out
  "ended elsewhere". After the sign-out the wipe re-rendered Now and Day, whose calendar reads (and,
  on a start with a revoked token, the bootstrap's pull and notification fetch) went out with no
  token at all: each a 401 and a console error. Ending "This device" from the list had been fixed
  for the socket (fix round 1 of QA round 15: the socket rests first); Log out was not swept with
  it. The reported "ended on another device" after a reload was the QA script's: its init script
  put the revoked token back on every load. **Missed because** the logout tests had no socket and
  no screen mounted, the e2e logout checked only the URL and the heading, and no test counted the
  requests after a sign-out. **Rule.** A device forgets its session before it tells the server
  (`logout`: sign out locally, then `POST /api/auth/logout` with the token it had, through
  `call(…, { credential })`); and once a session of this device has ended (`auth.hasEnded()`: by
  hand, from the list, by a 401), a call whose contract needs one (`auth: true`, the flag the
  Worker's `mount` guards with) is not sent without a token: the API client answers it with the
  server's own 401 `auth/unauthorized` locally. (A device that never had a session reaches no
  screen that makes such calls: the route gate. The app's screen tests render signed-in screens on
  a signed-out runtime, so the stricter "never without a token" would need that fixture rebuilt.) Server side traced: `deleteSession` still closes the
  sockets and answers the logout; the logout route still needs the bearer; nothing else changed.
  **Sweep.** Every way a session ends here: Log out (fixed), ending this device from the list (the
  socket rests first, then `forget`), account deletion and a remote revoke (`forget` / `me`'s 401,
  local only); after a session ended, every call that needs one stops in the client (the
  calendar reads, the sync, the notification feed, usage). **Guard.** Client tests: the logout carries the old token
  and is the only request after it, the device is signed out before it leaves, a 4401 during it
  checks nothing; once the session ended, a call needing one is not sent; app tests: after Log out only
  the logout names the session, a start refused by a 401 asks nothing more; e2e (`login.e2e.ts`):
  after Log out on a lived-in account no request names the session but the logout, no 4xx, no
  console error, and a reload shows no "ended elsewhere"; `loginViaApi` puts the token in once per
  tab and `docs/qa.md` tells QA scripts to do the same.
- **Fix round 1 of QA 22 (review): an answer that writes auth state checks the token it was sent
  with.** With Log out signing out first, a session check (`me`) already on its way (the start-up
  refresh, the 4401 check, `adoptToken`) answered 200 after the logout and set `signed-in` again,
  with no token, on the wiped store: `me`'s failure branch compared the token, its success branch
  did not. Now both apply only while `session.token()` is still the one sent (else
  `session/changed`, nothing written), and `adoptToken` drops a refused token only while no newer
  login holds the device. Sweep of every auth-state writer: `meOf` (both branches), `adoptToken`,
  the 4401 handler (acts only on `me`'s answer), the logins (they mint a new token: writing it is
  their purpose), `forget`/`finishAccountDeletion`/`logout` (local, no answer to wait for). The same
  gap at the data layer: a first pull on its way at Log out wrote the old account's page, store id
  and cursor into the wiped copy; a pull now remembers the local copy it asked for
  (`state.copy()`, bumped the moment `clear` is called) and drops a page for a copy that is gone
  (`sync-pages.ts`). Guards: `auth.test.ts` (a held `me` released after logout or after another
  login changes nothing; `hasEnded` resets on login and adopt), `sync-client.test.ts` (a held first
  pull released after `clear` writes nothing).

### 49. Text a client chose shown as what the server saw
- **Bug.** Review of the sign-in notices (phase C, Task 5): a deep-link nonce is minted by whoever asks for it, with any `deviceName`. Someone who gets a person to open their link would get a session on that person's account, and a notice that opened with "You signed in from Pixel 9a" would vouch for it in words the stranger chose.
- **Missed because.** The notice was specified from the person's own logins, where the name is true; no test minted a nonce with a misleading or markup-laden name, and none asked which parts of the text the server observed and which the client claimed.
- **Rule.** A message that asks for a security decision ("Not me") leads with what the server observed (the address and place Cloudflare reports, never forgeable by the client), then the name the client gave. Client text goes as plain text (no `parse_mode` anywhere, in one `messageBody`), and identity is never merged by a client-chosen id (`deviceId` only files usage). A part the server did not observe (no `CF-Connecting-IP` off Cloudflare's edge) is left out, not guessed. Test with a name full of markup and check it arrives literally.

### 50. A schema change shipped in the release that stops using what it drops
- **Bug.** Final review of phase C: migration 0008 dropped `sessions.label` in the release whose Worker stopped reading it. `deploy.yml` migrates D1 before the new Worker serves, and `wrangler rollback` runs the previous Worker on the new schema; that Worker selects and inserts `label`, so every signed-in call and every login would have answered 500 from the migrate step until the new Worker served, and for good after a rollback.
- **Missed because.** The migration test checked the data (old rows get a kind and a name), never that the previous release's queries still run on the new schema; no test or check knew the order of the deploy's steps. It was the first destructive D1 migration.
- **Rule.** Expand, then contract: a release adds the new columns and keeps writing the old one (`legacyLabelOf`); a later release, once one has served without reading it, drops it with a `-- contract: <what stopped using it, and when>` line. `bun db:check` (`scripts/check-migrations.ts`) refuses a `DROP TABLE`/`DROP COLUMN`/`RENAME` without that line, in D1 and in the DO migrations (a rollback runs old DO code on a migrated store too). The migration test runs the previous release's insert and select after the migration. The follow-up is named in docs/architecture.md ("Schema changes ship expand, then contract"). A DO class added or removed (a `migrations` entry in `wrangler.jsonc`) also ends rollback past that release: say so in the release notes, and ship a new class alone first when the release around it must stay rollback-able (docs/architecture.md, "A Durable Object class added or removed is a rollback barrier"; found by the round-7 review, `LlmHealth`).

### 51. A client "make it so" loop against a server that silently refuses part of it
- **Bug.** QA round 15 H1: the feed marked every unread notice read, optimistically. The server skips a session's own sign-in notice on read (class 47) and answers `ok`; its `read` broadcast made the feed refresh, the notice came back unread, the effect marked it again: ~70 `POST /api/notifications/read` plus a `GET` per second for as long as the feed was open, and the bell never cleared.
- **Missed because.** The feed's tests used a fake server that marks whatever it is asked to; no test had a notice the server will not mark, and none counted requests over time. The server rule and the client effect were each right on their own.
- **Rule.** What the server will never do for a reader is shown to that reader as done: the list and the live notice give a session its own sign-in notice as read (`seenBy`, one core function beside `isOwnSignIn`), nothing is written, so the person's other devices still see it unread. And a client effect that asks the server to make something so asks once per item (`markRead` remembers what it asked and asks again only after a failed request), so a partial refusal can never loop with the refresh it sets off. Test with a server that refuses part and count the requests (client, app and e2e).

### 52. A goal already reached reported as a failure
- **Bug.** QA round 15 M1: ending a session that was already ended elsewhere answered 404 `session/not-found`, and Settings said "Couldn't end the session. Try again." for good, with the stale row and its confirm still there.
- **Missed because.** The revoke tests had a server holding every session listed; none ended one from another device between the list and the press.
- **Rule.** An action whose goal is "this is gone" succeeds when it is already gone: the row goes and the list reloads (it was stale). The server keeps its 404 (other clients read it), the client reads that code as done (`isAlreadyEnded` in `useSessions`). Sweep: the other client calls that end something are idempotent already (a resolved notification is answered as it stands, Not me on an ended session says it ended, unsubscribing nothing is `ok`).

### 53. A value the system filled in kept as if the person chose it
- **Bug.** QA rounds 11 M3, 13 and 14 L10e: a series made without a start counted its instances from the minute it was made, so every later instance "started" at 22:16, a time nobody picked ("Starts tomorrow 22:16 · Due Nov 4 23:59").
- **Missed because.** Every series fixture had an explicit start at a round hour (09:00); none was made without a start, at an odd minute.
- **Rule.** Where an action fills in a field the person left empty (`?? now`), the filled value serves only its own purpose and is never carried forward as the person's choice: a series counts from `seriesAnchor` (the chosen start, else 00:00 of the day it was made). Test the "left empty" path with a fast-check property over the minute it happens, in Europe/Moscow. Sweep: the two series starts (New task with a repeat, a repeat added in Edit) were the only `?? clock.now()` carried into later data; a plain task keeps no start of its own (round 14 L3), an activity's `stillAt` is the moment itself.

### 54. A test steps a platform timer by hand while the platform still runs it
- **Bug.** CI on PR #48: the sleep alarm test read no alarm at all after firing sleep's due look (`expected 0 to be greater than or equal to …`). The store was fine: the test had set the alarm one second in the past, so workerd's own scheduler fired it at once, alongside `runDurableObjectAlarm`. The helper then found no alarm, ran nothing and said so (`false`, unchecked), and the test read the alarm while the runtime's run was still in its pass, before it armed the next look. A loop of 60 such steps left the alarm unset at that read 3 to 4 times; a moment later it was always set again.
- **Missed because.** The test passed whenever the helper won the race, which it did on every local run. Its fixtures were also built from the real clock, so the race window moved with the time of day.
- **Rule.** A test that fires a Durable Object's alarm by hand keeps every alarm the store can arm ahead of the real clock (freeze `Date` far ahead with `vi.setSystemTime`, or give the store instants decades on) and asserts that the alarm it fires was there (`fireAlarm` in `sleep.int.test.ts`). Never `setAlarm(Date.now() - …)` against a live store. Sweep: `sleep.int.test.ts` and `sleep-pass.test.ts` now run on 2099 instants. The other tests that arm alarms at past fixed instants (`notify.int.test.ts`, `projections.int.test.ts`) never fire one by hand and do their work inside one `runInDurableObject` callback; the runtime's run of their alarm sends through the stubbed Telegram (`tests/setup.ts`) and so far changed no assertion. They are the next place to look if a notify test flickers.

### 55. A hot path whose cost grows with all history
- **Bug.** Whole-branch review I2: every sleep pass (each phone upload, each night alarm, about 150 a day) read the whole `usage_sessions` table (`end_at > ?` had no index, `SCAN usage_sessions`), and every phone upload loaded the whole event log because `knownUntil` moved and so the digest always changed. Nothing is pruned (the owner keeps the data), so the Workers Free daily reads would run out within months.
- **Missed because.** Tests ran against stores with a few rows; nothing measured rows read or the query plan, and the digest guard was checked with a host whose `knownUntil` did not move. Then (re-review I-1 of fix wave 10–15) the same guard was checked with hosts that answered every use whatever `from` they were asked for, so the window's front never moved: in production it moves with every pass, at night through the previous day's use, and the digest changed on almost every pass. Then (re-review I-1 of fix wave 10–15, round 2) the hold saved nothing in production at all: a Durable Object idle for a minute or two is evicted, so between alarms 15 minutes apart every alarm and upload started on a cold instance, and the sleep host's `zone()` materialized the whole log to read one setting, the alarm ran the pool pass (whose `derive` materializes it) at every sleep look, and every cold rebuild deleted and rewrote every projection row. The fake hosts stubbed `zone`, and the workerd test kept one warm instance all night, so the cache always hit. Nothing counted loads or rows written on a cold instance.
- **Rule.** A read on a path that runs per upload, per alarm or per pass is bounded by its window, through an index on the bound it filters (check with `EXPLAIN QUERY PLAN` and the cursor's `rowsRead` in a test with months of history). An `ORDER BY` on another indexed column can send SQLite back to a full index scan: sort in code. A guard that skips work compares only what the work can depend on, and what changes on every call (like `knownUntil`, or a window's front that moves with `now`) is compared against the next instant it matters (`sleepHorizon`) or held still while the guard lasts (the hold's `anchor`, read from instead of the moving front, let go a day on), with a property that a pass inside the guard decides nothing wherever the front is. A fake of a windowed read answers only what its window holds, and a cost test runs over a whole night of passes with a full day of history before it. A cost test runs on a cold instance (a new `UserStore` over the same storage for every call, as production evicts between alarms) and counts whole-log loads and rows written with `meterSql`; a fake host that stubs a read (`zone`) the real host makes through the costly path hides exactly this. Every wait that shares an alarm reads nothing before its own time (`poolAtAlarm`, the queue's due text, `sleepAtAlarm`); a table derived from the log carries a mark of the log `seq` and build it mirrors, so a cold start writes nothing, and a rebuild after an out-of-order write rewrites only the rows that differ; an upload that a client sends again unchanged (the calendar copy) writes no row and wakes nothing. Rows kept up incrementally no longer heal at every cold start, so a property pins them: any writes, in order or not, corrections included, leave the same rows as every row written from scratch (`projections-diff.int.test.ts`).
- **Task 2.6 (2026-11-02), the client side: a full rebuild was quadratic.** Found by Task 2.5's measurements: rebuilding the state from the log (app start, Undo, delete, amend, a retro action, a refused write's rollback, and the Worker's cold `materialize`) took 0.3 s for 2,000 events and 6 s for 8,000 (Node; a phone at 4× is worse). Every reducer that keeps a map copied all of it for each event it folded (`{ ...state.activities, [id]: … }`, the same in tasks, projects, presets and series), and every activity start mapped over every activity ever to close the running one. **Missed because.** Every reducer and materializer test folds a few to a few dozen events, where a whole copy per event costs nothing; the one timed test (`materializes 20k events under 200 ms`) used a reducer that keeps a number, not a map, and nothing timed a fold of the real reducers over a log of a year's length. **Fix.** `materialize` runs its fold as one batch (`materialize/transient.ts`): a map the batch made is written in place, every other one (the shared `INITIAL_*`, a state from outside, anything after the batch) is copied as before, and the batch's maps are frozen before it answers; an activity start reads an index of the open main activities that the batch keeps. Incremental integrate runs outside a batch and copies, as it always did. **Rule (added).** A reducer writes a map entry only through `assoc`/`putById` and reads a subset of a map only through `keysWhere` with an index, never by scanning the map for each event; anything else that folds a whole log keeps its per-event cost independent of the log's length. **Guard.** `materialize-cost.test.ts`: a seeded year of the owner's use (`yearOfUse`: starts, stops, relabels, adjustments, alongside activities, logged blocks and calendar events, tasks with checks, closes and reopens, projects, series, amendments, revocations) materialized at 2,000 and 8,000 events, 8k at most 5× 2k and within 300 ms; and properties that the batch equals the reducers folded one event at a time (tangled logs with corrections, years of any length, `materializeAt` as of any instant), that it never changes the initial state, and that an event integrated afterwards leaves the rebuilt state as it was. The oracle's main start closes by its own scan of every activity, so a bug in the shared index predicate fails the properties (checked by breaking it). **Swept, per action (fix round 1).** The client's incremental `applyInOrder` sorted the whole `events` and `log` again on every action (`sortEvents([...state.events, ...fresh])`): `mergeEvents` (core `events/sort.ts`) now finds where the fresh events go by binary search and copies, sorting only what comes after the first of them (nothing, for an action at the end), with the same order as the sort, ties included (fast-check property, element for element). Guards: a comparison count (appending one event to 8,000 compares at most 20 times; the sort compared about 8,000) and `state-cost.test.ts` (an action's time on a year of 8,000 events at most 5× that on 2,000, within a 16 ms frame; 4.5–5.6 ms before, 3.4 ms after under coverage). The rest of an action's cost is the reducers' one map copy per event outside a batch, linear in the map and left as it is. A batch nested in another now freezes the outer one's maps when it starts, so a state the inner batch answers unchanged can no longer be written afterwards by the outer one.

### 56. A record re-derived from a sliding window after the window dropped its grounds
- **Bug.** Found while fixing re-review I-1 of fix wave 10–15: automatic sleep keeps a night it opened only while deciding afresh would open the same one (`reconcile`). Deciding afresh finds a night's start after the last use before it, but the pass reads use from a window that slides 36 hours behind `now`. When the front passed between that use and the start (a phone put away at 19:52 and a night from 21:00: for up to nine hours a day; at the very minute otherwise), the night was still followed and its grounds were gone, so deciding afresh opened nothing and the pass discarded a real night 36 hours after it was decided.
- **Missed because.** Every rules test and property passed the same `usesFrom` (noon before the night) to every pass; nothing moved the window's front, and the store's test hosts answered every use whatever `from` they were asked for.
- **Rule.** A rule that re-derives a record from windowed data and revokes it when the derivation fails first checks that the window still holds what the record was derived from; when it does not, the record stands as decided (`isOwned` asks for a known use at or before the start; such a sleep is walked as it stands and keeps its night). Property 13 "decides nothing more as the window's front moves on" moves the front anywhere short of what is known and asserts nothing is decided. Fakes of a windowed read honour the window (class 55).

### 57. A skip gate that can wait forever
- **Bug.** Re-review I-1 of fix wave 10–15, round 3: the store's alarm passes the notification pool only once its own time (`pool:next-at`) has come, and a pass that saw nothing coming (no digest window, no event within the three days of calendar it reads) recorded `null`, "never". Only a write or a changed calendar planned the pool again, so with digests off an event four days out (the return flight after a week away) entered the window while the phone sent the same calendar copy every 15 minutes, and was never announced. A deploy that changed the pool's rules would have left such a store on the old plan the same way.
- **Missed because.** The gate was added to stop a log load at every alarm, and its tests asked only whether it skips (no load before the time) and passes at its time; none left a store quiet for days with an input that moves on its own. The inputs that used to re-plan the pool by accident (an `afterWrite` on every calendar upload, the pool at every sleep look) were exactly the cost being removed, and nothing replaced them.
- **Rule.** A gate that skips work until a recorded time has an upper bound: never later than the shortest horizon its inputs are read over (the pool's calendar window is three days, it passes at least daily: `LOOK_AGAIN_MS`), so a sliding window, a clock-driven input or new rules after a deploy are looked at again within it. "Nothing coming" is never stored as "never". Test it on a quiet store: no write, the copy sent again unchanged, days of alarms on a cold instance, and the thing still happens at its instant (`night-cost.int.test.ts` "looks again within a day"). The bound must also start on its own: a gate whose time only the gated work writes is checked from the state the previous build leaves a store in (no key, no wake armed), and anything that reaches such a store with neither arms the work at once (`armIfPoolUnplanned`, on every write and every calendar copy, changed or not); a test that builds that state by hand (deletes the key and the alarm) is the only one that sees it, since a store made by the new code always has a pass behind it.

### 58. A claim saved before the work it stands for, released only by a thrown error
- **Bug.** Re-review I-2 of fix wave 10–15, round 3: a pool pass took the pool's time by storing `pool:next-at = null` before it ran, and only its `catch` gave the time back. A pass whose isolate was reset while it waited on a delivery (every deploy resets every Durable Object; the CPU limit; a machine move) never ran the `catch`, the output gate had already made the `null` durable, and Cloudflare's own retry of the alarm read `null` as "nothing coming" and skipped. A digest or an announcement due then waited for the person's next write.
- **Missed because.** The failure tests made the pass throw, which always reaches the `catch`; nothing stopped a pass midway without an exception. A kill and a throw look alike in a diff and differ only at runtime.
- **Rule.** A marker that makes a later run skip work (taken, done, seen, nothing coming) is written only after the work it stands for, or as a lease: the time it runs out at, honoured by the gate, given back at the end only if it is still the one held, and kept apart from what writes during the work ask for (`pool:lease` beside `pool:next-at`, so a write's later time never hides a pass that died), with its own wake armed at its end so the retry never depends on the platform running the work again. That wake must cost a gate read and no more after work that completed, and work that keeps failing backs off instead of rerunning at every lease's end; the attempt is counted when the claim is taken, before the work (`pool:failures`, cleared only by work that completes), so a kill backs off as a throw does: the lease itself lengthens with the count (5 minutes doubling to four hours) and a throw waits a minute doubling to four hours. A count kept only in the `catch` has the same blind spot as a claim released only there. The work asks for its own next run only at its end: a time it asks for on the way (a failed delivery's "again in a minute") stands after it breaks, and the backoff, which keeps an earlier time a write asked for, takes it as one.
